Stability of the Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg inequality along Felli-Schneider curve: critical points at infinity
Juncheng Wei, Yuanze Wu

Abstract
. In this paper, we consider the following Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg (CKN for short) inequality ( ∫ ℝ d | x | − b ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ​ | u | p + 1 ​ 𝑑 x ) 2 p + 1 ≤ S a , b ​ ∫ ℝ d | x | − 2 ​ a ​ | ∇ u | 2 ​ 𝑑 x , \displaystyle\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p+1}dx\bigg)^{\frac{2}{p+1}}\leq S_{a,b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-2a}|\nabla u|^{2}dx, where u ∈ D a 1 , 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , p = d + 2 ​ ( 1 + a − b ) d − 2 ​ ( 1 + a − b ) p=\frac{d+2(1+a-b)}{d-2(1+a-b)} and { a < b < a + 1 , d = 2 , a ≤ b < a + 1 , d ≥ 3 . \displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &a<b<a+1,\quad d=2,\\ &a\leq b<a+1,\quad d\geq 3.\end{aligned}\right. (0.1) Based on the ideas of [ 22 , 53 ] , we develop a suitable strategy to derive the following sharp stability of the critical points at infinity of the above CKN inequality in the degenerate case d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , b = b F ​ S ​ ( a ) b=b_{FS}(a) (Felli-Schneider curve) and a < 0 a<0 : let ν ∈ ℕ \nu\in{\mathbb{N}} and u ∈ D a 1 , 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) be an nonnegative function such that ( ν − 1 2 ) ​ ( S a , b − 1 ) p + 1 p − 1 < ‖ u ‖ D a 1 , 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) 2 < ( ν + 1 2 ) ​ ( S a , b − 1 ) p + 1 p − 1 \displaystyle\left(\nu-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(S_{a,b}^{-1}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}<\|u\|^{2}_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(S_{a,b}^{-1}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}} (0.2) Then inf α → ν ∈ ( ℝ + ) ν , λ → ν ∈ ℝ ν ‖ u − ∑ j = 1 ν α j W λ j ‖ ≲ ‖ − d i v ( | x | − a ∇ u ) − | x | − b ⁡ ( p + 1 ) | u | p − 1 u ‖ W a − 1 , 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) 1 3 \displaystyle\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{\lambda}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|u-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}W_{\lambda_{j}}\right\|\lesssim\left\|-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)-|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u\right\|_{W^{-1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{3}} as ‖ − d i v ( | x | − a ∇ u ) − | x | − b ⁡ ( p + 1 ) | u | p − 1 u ‖ W a − 1 , 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) → 0 \left\|-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)-|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u\right\|_{W^{-1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0 , where b F ​ S ​ ( a ) b_{FS}(a) is the well known Felli-Schneider curve. The above stability is sharp in the sense that the order of the right hand side can not be improved. The significant finding in our result is that in the degenerate case, the power of the optimal stability is an absolute constant 1 / 3 1/3 (independent of p p and ν \nu ) which is quite different from the non-degenerate case [ 22 , 78 ] . We also believe that our strategy of proofs will be very useful in studying many other degenerate problems. Keywords: Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg inequality; Sharp stability; the Felli-Schneider curve; Critical points at infinity. AMS Subject Classification 2010: 35B09; 35B33; 35B40; 35J20.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and Previous Results.

Let
d\geq 2
be a positive integer and
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the Hilbert space given by

\displaystyle D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\left\{u\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-2a}|\nabla u|^{2}dx<+\infty\right\}

(1.1)

with the inner product

\displaystyle\langle u,v\rangle_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-2a}\nabla u\nabla vdx

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, where
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\dot{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the usual homogeneous Sobolev space (cf.
[
40
, Definition 2.1]
) with
D^{-1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
being the dual space. Then the following Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg (CKN for short in what follows) inequality

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p+1}dx\bigg)^{\frac{2}{p+1}}\leq S_{a,b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-2a}|\nabla u|^{2}dx,

(1.2)

which is established by Caffarelli, Kohn and Nirenberg in the celebrated paper
[
12
]
in a more general version,
holds for all
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
where
d\geq 2
,
p=\frac{d+2(1+a-b)}{d-2(1+a-b)}
and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &a<b<a+1,\quad d=2,\\
&a\leq b<a+1,\quad d\geq 3.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

Here, for the sake of simplicity, we denote
a_{c}=\frac{d-2}{2}
, as in
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
.

As pointed out by Catrina and Wang in
[
13
]
, a fundamental task in understanding a functional inequality is to study the best constants, existence (and nonexistence) of extremal functions, as well as their qualitative properties and classifications, which have played important roles in many applications by virtue of the complete knowledge on the minimizers. For the CKN inequality (
1.2
), it is known that up to dilations
u_{\tau}(x)=\tau^{a_{c}-a}u(\tau x)
and scalar multiplications
Cu(x)
(also up to translations
u(x+y)
for the spacial case
a=b=0
), the radial function
W(x)
given by

\displaystyle W(x)=\left(2(p+1)(a_{c}-a)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{(p-1)}}\bigg(1+|x|^{(a_{c}-a)(p-1)}\bigg)^{-\frac{2}{p-1}}

(1.4)

is the unique nonnegative solution of (
2.2
) in
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
d\geq 2
under the conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &b_{FS}(a)\leq b<a+1,\quad a<0,\\
&a\leq b<a+1,\quad a\geq 0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.5)

where

\displaystyle b_{FS}(a)=\frac{d(a_{c}-a)}{2\sqrt{(a_{c}-a)^{2}+(d-1)}}+a-a_{c}>a

is the well known Felli-Schneider curve found in
[
49
]
. Precisely, Aubin and Talanti classified the extremal functions of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) for
a=b=0
in
[
3
,
76
]
, respectively. As a special case, Lieb classified the extremal functions of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) for
a=0
and
0<b<1
in
[
63
]
. Chou and Chu classified the extremal functions of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) for
a\geq 0
in
[
18
]
. Felli and Schneider proved in
[
49
]
that extremal functions of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) must be nonradial if
a<0
and
a<b<b_{FS}(a)
. Dolbeault, Esteban, Loss and Tarantello finally classified the extremal functions of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) in
[
31
,
32
]
under the conditions (
1.5
). Catrina and Wang proved in
[
65
]
that extremal functions of (
1.2
) must have
\mathcal{O}(N-1)
symmetry for
a<b<b_{FS}(a)
with
a<0
.
Moreover, it is also well known that
W(x)
is nondegenerate in
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
under the condition (
1.5
) except
b=b_{FS}(a)
(cf.
[
49
]
). That is, up to scalar multiplications
CV(x)
,

\displaystyle V(x):=\nabla W(x)\cdot x-(a_{c}-a)W(x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left(\lambda^{-(a_{c}-a)}W(\lambda x)\right)|_{\lambda=1}

(1.6)

is the only nonzero solution in
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the linearization of (
2.2
) around
W
which is given by

\displaystyle-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)=p|x|^{-b(p+1)}W^{p-1}u,\quad u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(1.7)

However, if the parameters
a
and
b
lie on the Felli-Schneider curve, that is,
b=b_{FS}(a)
with
a<0
, then the bubble
W(x)
is degenerate in
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf.
[
53
]
). For the sake of simplicity, we introduce the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\left\{cW_{\tau}(x)\mid c\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}\text{ and }\tau>0\right\}

and the usual weighted Lebesgue space
L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p+1}dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p+1}}.

in what follows.

As pointed out by Dolbeault and Esteban in
[
27
]
(see also Figalli in
[
39
]
), once optimal constants are known and the set of extremal functions has been characterised, the next question is to understand stability: which kind of distance is measured by the deficit, that is, the difference of the two terms in the functional inequality, written with the optimal constant. These studies were initialed by Brezis and Lieb in
[
8
]
by raising an open question for the classical Sobolev inequality,

\displaystyle S\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u|^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}dx\bigg)^{\frac{d-2}{d}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla u|^{2}dx,\quad u\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(1.8)

which was settled partially by Egnell-Pacella-Tricarico in
[
35
]
and completely by Bianchi-Egnell in
[
5
]
by proving that

\displaystyle 0<s_{BE}=\inf_{u\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-S\|u\|^{2}_{L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{dist_{D^{1,2}}^{2}(u,\mathcal{M})},

(1.9)

where
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is the usual norm in the Lebesgue space
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\{U_{\lambda,z,c}\mid(\lambda,z,c)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\}.

Due to the non-Hilbert property of
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
p\not=2
, the generalization of the Bianchi-Egnell stability (
1.9
) to the general
L^{p}
-Sobolev inequality takes a long time to introduce new ideas and develop new techniques by Cianchi in
[
19
]
, Cianchi-Fusco-Maggi-Pratelli in
[
20
]
, Figalli-Magggi-Pratelli in
[
46
]
, Figalli-Neumayer in
[
47
]
, Fusco in
[
55
]
, Fusco-Maggi-Pratelli in
[
56
]
, Neumayer in
[
69
]
and finally, Figalli and Zhang proved the optimal Bianchi-Egnell stability of the general
L^{p}
-Sobolev inequality in
[
48
]
. The Bianchi-Egnell type stability like (
1.9
) was also generalized to many other famous inequalities. Since the literature on this topic is so vast and this direction is not the main topic in our paper, we only refer the readers to
[
9
,
16
,
26
,
33
]
for the Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev inequality,
[
34
,
67
,
70
,
72
,
74
]
for the Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality,
[
7
,
17
,
36
,
38
,
50
,
57
,
58
,
80
]
for the logarithmic Sobolev inequality,
[
1
,
14
,
24
,
53
,
77
,
78
]
for the Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg inequality,
[
4
,
6
,
15
,
52
,
66
]
for other kinds of Sobolev inequalities and
[
11
,
23
,
37
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
54
,
59
,
68
,
71
]
for many kinds of geometric inequalities. We would like to highlight the survey
[
27
]
and the Lecture notes
[
39
,
51
]
to the readers for their detailed introductions and references about the studies on the stability of inequalities.
In particular, the Bianchi-Egnell type stability of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) reads as follows:

(1)

The nondegenerate case (
[
78
,
79
]
). Let
d\geq 2
and either

(i)

b_{FS}(a)<b<a+1
with
a<0
or

(ii)

a\leq b<a+1
with
a\geq 0
and
a+b>0
.

Then

\displaystyle 0<c_{BE}=\inf_{u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-S_{a,b}^{-1}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{dist_{D^{1,2}_{a}}^{2}(u,\mathcal{Z})}

for all
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(2)

The degenerate case (
[
53
]
).  Let
d\geq 2
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
with
a<0
. Then

\displaystyle 0<c_{BE}=\inf_{u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\backslash\mathcal{Z}}\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left(\|u\|^{2}_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-S_{a,b}^{-1}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)}{dist^{4}(u,\mathcal{Z})}

for all
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We remark that Bianchi and Egnell’s arguments for (
1.9
) depends on the nondegeneracy of the Talanti bubble
U
in
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thus, to establish the Bianchi-Egnell type stability of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) in the degenerate case, Frank and Peteranderl
[
53
]
introduced new idea and developed new techniques to expand the deficit of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) up to the fourth order terms in
[
53
]
, as that in
[
52
]
. We would like to mention the paper
[
10
]
where Carlen and Figalli proved a quantitative convergence result for the critical mass Keller-Segel system by the Bianchi-Egnell type stability of Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality and the logarithmic Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev inequality, which provides the potential applications of the studies on the stability of many other inequalities. We also want to mention the paper
[
9
]
, where Carlen develop a dual method to establish stability of functional inequalities. Finally we want to mention is that in the very recent papers
[
60
,
61
,
62
]
, Konig proved that
s_{BE}
is attainable which gives a positive answer to the open question proposed by Dolbeault, Esteban, Figalli, Frank and Loss in
[
28
]
and makes the key step in answering the long-standing open question of determining the best constant
s_{BE}
. Konig’s result on
s_{BE}
has been generalized to
c_{BE}
in the nondegenerate case in our very recent paper
[
79
]
.

On the other hand, it is well known that all critical points at infinity
of the corresponding functional of the Sobolev inequality (
1.8
) are induced by limits of sums of Talenti bubbles (at least if we
consider only nonnegative functions) which can be precisely stated as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Struwe
[
75
]
) Let
d\geq 3
and
\nu\geq 1
be positive integers. Let
\{u_{n}\}\subset D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a nonnegative sequence with

\displaystyle(\nu-\frac{1}{2})S^{\frac{d}{2}}<\|u_{n}\|_{D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}<(\nu+\frac{1}{2})S^{\frac{d}{2}},

where
S
is the best Sobolev constant.
Assume that
\|\Delta u_{n}+|u_{n}|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u_{n}\|_{D^{-1,2}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then there exist a sequence
(z_{1}^{(n)},z_{2}^{(n)},\cdots,z_{\nu}^{(n)})
of
\nu
-tuples of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a sequence of
(\lambda_{1}^{(n)},\lambda_{2}^{(n)},\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}^{(n)})
of
\nu
-tuples of positive real numbers such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{n}-\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\nabla U[z_{i}^{(n)},\lambda_{i}^{(n)}]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Based on the above well-known Struwe decomposition, Ciraolo, Figalli, Glaudo and Maggi proposed the following question on the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the Sobolev inequality (
1.8
) at infinity:

(Q)

Let
d\geq 3
and
\nu\geq 1
be positive integers. If
\{u\}\subset D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be nonnegative,

\displaystyle(\nu-\frac{1}{2})S^{\frac{d}{2}}<\|u\|_{D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}<(\nu+\frac{1}{2})S^{\frac{d}{2}}

and
\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u\|_{D^{-1,2}}<<1
, does there exist a constant
C(d,\nu)
such that

\displaystyle dist_{D^{1,2}}(u,\mathcal{M})\leq C(d,\nu)\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{N-2}}u\|_{D^{-1,2}}?

Remark 1.1
.

The original question (
[
40
, Problem 1.2]
) is more general than
(Q)
stated here in the sense that,
u
could be sign-changing if
u
is close to the sum of
U[z_{i},\lambda_{i}]
in
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where
U[z_{i},\lambda_{i}]
are weakly interacting (the definition of weakly interaction can be found in
[
40
, Definition 3.1]
). We choose to state the question
(Q)
since it is more close to Theorem
1.1
(Struwe
[
75
]
).

In the recent papers
[
21
,
40
]
, Ciraolo, Figalli, Glaudo and Maggi proved the following results by the energy method:

(1)

(Ciraolo-Figalli-Maggi
[
21
]
) Let
d\geq 3
and
u\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be positive such that
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}S^{\frac{d}{2}}
and
\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u\|_{D^{-1,2}}\leq\delta
for some
\delta>0
sufficiently small, then
dist_{D^{1,2}}(u,\mathcal{M})\lesssim\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u\|_{d^{-1,2}}
.

(2)

(Figalli-Glaudo
[
40
]
) Let
u\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be nonnegative such that

\displaystyle(\nu-\frac{1}{2})S^{\frac{d}{2}}<\|u\|_{D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}<(\nu+\frac{1}{2})S^{\frac{d}{2}}

and
\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u\|_{D^{-1,2}}\leq\delta
for some
\delta>0
sufficiently small, then
dist_{D^{1,2}}(u,\mathcal{M}^{\nu})\lesssim\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u\|_{D^{-1,2}}
for
3\leq N\leq 5
, where

\mathcal{M}^{\nu}=\{(U[z_{1},\lambda_{1}],U[z_{2},\lambda_{2}],\cdots,U[z_{\nu},\lambda_{\nu}])\mid z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\lambda_{i}>0\}
.

It is worth pointing out that a significant finding in
[
40
]
is that Figalli and Glaudo construct a counterexample for
\nu=2
and
d\geq 6
to show that the answer of the question
(Q)
for
\nu\geq 2
and
d\geq 6
is negative! Based on their counterexample for
d\geq 6
, Figalli and Glaudo conjectured in
[
40
]
that the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the Sobolev inequality (
1.8
) at infinity should be of the following nonlinear form:

\displaystyle dist_{D^{1,2}}(u,\mathcal{M}^{\nu})\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\|\Delta u+|u|u\|_{H^{-1}}|\ln(\|\Delta u+|u|u\|_{D^{-1,2}})|,\quad\nu\geq 2\text{ and }d=6;\\
&\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{N-2}}u\|_{D^{-1,2}}^{\gamma(d)},\quad\nu\geq 2\text{ and }d\geq 7\end{aligned}\right.

with
0<\gamma(d)<1
. We would like to point out that, besides its own mathematical interests, the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the Sobolev inequality (
1.8
) at infinity can be used to prove quantitative convergence results for the fast diffusion equation, see, for example
[
21
,
40
]
. In the very recent work
[
22
]
, the first author, together with Deng and Sun, proved that tthe stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the Sobolev inequality (
1.8
) at infinity is actually of the following nonlinear form by combining the energy method, the reduction argument and the blow-up analysis:

\displaystyle dist_{D^{1,2}}(u,\mathcal{M}^{\nu})\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\|\Delta u+|u|u\|_{D^{-1,2}}|\ln(\|\Delta u+|u|u\|_{D^{-1,2}})|^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \ \nu\geq 2\text{ and }d=6;\\
&\|\Delta u+|u|^{\frac{4}{d-2}}u\|_{H^{-1}}^{\frac{d+2}{2(d-2)}},\ \ \nu\geq 2\text{ and }d\geq 7.\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover, the orders of the right hand sides in above estimates are shown to be optimal by constructing related examples. We remark that due to the mathematical interests and potential applications, the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of other famous functional inequalities at infinity have already been carried out, see, for example by Aryan in
[
2
]
and De Nitti and Konig in
[
25
]
for the fractional Sobolev inequality, and by us in
[
78
]
for the CKN inequality (
1.2
) in the nondegenerate case. In particular, the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) at infinity is stated as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
d\geq 2
and
\nu\geq 1
be positive integers and either

(i)

b_{FS}(a)<b<a+1
with
a<0
or

(ii)

a\leq b<a+1
with
a\geq 0
and
a+b>0
.

Then for any nonnegative
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle(\nu-\frac{1}{2})(S_{a,b}^{-1})^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}<\|u\|_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}<(\nu+\frac{1}{2})(S_{a,b}^{-1})^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}

and
\Gamma(u)\leq\delta
with some
\delta>0
sufficiently small,
we have

\displaystyle dist_{D_{a}^{1,2}}(u,\mathcal{Z}^{\nu})\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Gamma(u),\quad p>2\text{ or }\nu=1,\\
&\Gamma(u)|\log\Gamma(u)|^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad p=2\text{ and }\nu\geq 2,\\
&\Gamma(u)^{\frac{p}{2}},\quad 1<p<2\text{ and }\nu\geq 2,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\Gamma(u)=\|div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)+|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u\|_{D_{a}^{-1,2}}

and

\mathcal{Z}^{\nu}=\{(W_{\tau_{1}},W_{\tau_{2}},\cdots,W_{\tau_{\nu}})\mid\tau_{i}>0\}.

Moreover, the orders of the right hand sides in above estimates are sharp.

We remark that Theorem
1.2
is a direct generalization of the Ciraolo-Figalli-Maggi,
Figalli-Glaudo and Deng-Sun-Wei results in
[
21
,
40
,
22
]
for the Sobolev inequality
1.8
to the CKN inequality (
1.2
) in the nondegenerate case, which was mainly based on the following Struwe decomposition of critical points of the corresponding functional of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) at infinity.

Proposition 1.1
.

(
[
78
, Proposition 3.2]
or
[
13
, Lemma 4.2]
) Let
d\geq 2
and
\nu\geq 1
be positive integers and either

(i)

b_{FS}(a)<b<a+1
with
a<0
or

(ii)

a\leq b<a+1
with
a\geq 0
and
a+b>0
.

If
\{w_{n}\}
be a nonnegative sequence with

\displaystyle(\nu-\frac{1}{2})(S_{a,b}^{-1})^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}<\|w_{n}\|_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}<(\nu+\frac{1}{2})(S_{a,b}^{-1})^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}

Then there exists
\{\tau_{i,n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, satisfying

\displaystyle\min_{i\not=j}\bigg\{\max\bigg\{\frac{\tau_{i,n}}{\tau_{j,n}},\frac{\tau_{j,n}}{\tau_{i,n}}\bigg\}\bigg\}\to+\infty

as
n\to\infty
for
\nu\geq 2
, such that

(1)

w_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}(W)_{\tau_{i,n}}+o_{n}(1)
in
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(2)

\|w_{n}\|_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}=\nu\|W\|_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+o_{n}(1)
.

1.2.
Main result

Since the Struwe decomposition (Proposition
1.1
) of critical points of the corresponding functional of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) at infinity also holds in the degenerate case. It is natural to ask the following question:

(\mathcal{Q})

Does the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) at infinity hold true in the degenerate case?

We shall answer the natural question
(\mathcal{Q})
by proving the following sharp result.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Suppose
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a nonnegative function such that

\displaystyle\left(\nu-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(S_{a,b}^{-1}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}<\|u\|^{2}_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(S_{a,b}^{-1}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}.

(1.10)

(a)

Then as
\left\|-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)-|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u\right\|_{W^{-1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{\lambda}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|u-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}W_{\lambda_{j}}\right\|\lesssim\left\|-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)-|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u\right\|_{W^{-1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{3}}.

(b)

There exists
\{u_{n}\}\subset D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, nonnegative and satisfies (
1.10
), such that

\displaystyle\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{\lambda}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|u-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}W_{\lambda_{j}}\right\|\sim\left\|-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)-|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u\right\|_{W^{-1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Remark 1.2
.

In preparing this paper, we knew from personal communications with Professor W. Zou that their group was also working on the question
(\mathcal{Q})
for the one-bubble case. Moreover, we notice that in a very recent paper
[
81
]
, the optimal stability for the one-bubble case has been established by Zhou and Zou
[
81
]
, while for the multi-bubble case only a partial result is obtained. By assuming that the projection to the nontrivial kernel is much smaller than the interaction, they obtained the exponent
p/2
, which is the same as in the non-degenerate case Theorem
1.2
. Note that from Theorem
1.3
the most important contribution comes exactly from the projection to the nontrivial kernels.

Remark 1.3
.

Theorem
1.3
is rather surprising since the optimal power of the stability is an absolute constant
\frac{1}{3}
which is independent of
p
and
\nu
. This is a completely new finding in the studies on the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of functional inequalities at infinity. This new finding can be explained by the optimal example of the stability stated in Theorem
1.3
which is given in section 9. Roughly speaking, for the two-bubble case, the optimal power of the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) at infinity depends on two values, the interaction between bubbles which is measured by the distance of these bubbles and the projections of these bubbles on their nontrivial kernels. If the interaction wins the projections then the optimal power of the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional at infinity will be values in Theorem
1.2
which depends on
p
. If the projections wins the interaction then the optimal power of the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional at infinity will be the absolute constant
\frac{1}{3}
. If the projections and the interaction are comparable then the optimal power of the stability of critical points of the corresponding functional at infinity will lie between the values of Theorem
1.2
and the absolute constant
\frac{1}{3}
. We refer the readers to Remark
9.1
for more details. Since the function
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
discussed in Theorem
1.3
is arbitrary, the optimal power of the stability must be the absolute constant
\frac{1}{3}
.

1.3.
Sketch of the proof

The basic idea in proving Theorem
1.3
is still to apply the Deng-Sun-Wei arguments in
[
22
]
, as in
[
78
]
. Since the bubble
W
is degenerate now, we need also employ the Frank-Peteranderl strategy in
[
53
]
. However, since our problem is in the critical point setting, new ideas and new techniques are also needed to develop. Let us now explain our strategy in proving Theorem
1.3
in what follows.

In the first step, we need to set a good problem. Suppose that
u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a nonnegative function. We first transform the problem onto the cylinder
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, as usual. Then, based on the Struwe decomposition (Proposition
1.1
), the basic idea is to decompose
v
, the the image of the bubble
u
on the cylinder
\mathcal{C}
, into two parts, as in
[
21
,
40
,
22
]
, by considering the following minimizing problem

\displaystyle\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|v-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}\Psi_{s_{j}}\right\|^{2},

so that we can write
v=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}^{*}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}+\rho
where the remaining term
\rho
is orthogonal to
\{\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}\}
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
. Since the bubble
\Psi
which is the image of the bubble
W
on the cylinder
\mathcal{C}
is degenerate now, we need further decompose the remaining term
\rho
and further write

\displaystyle v=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}^{*}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{j,l}^{*}w_{j,l}\right)+\rho_{*},

where
\{w_{j,l}\}
are the nontrivial kernels of
\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}
and the remaining term
\rho_{*}
is orthogonal to
\{\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}\}
and
\{w_{j,l}\}
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
, as in
[
53
]
. Since we are in the critical point setting, the remaining term
\rho_{*}
will also satisfy an elliptic equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}(\rho_{*})=f+\mathcal{R}_{int}+\mathcal{N},\quad\text{in }\mathcal{C},\\
&\langle\Psi_{j},\rho_{*}\rangle=\langle\partial_{t}\Psi_{j},\rho_{*}\rangle=\langle w_{j,l},\rho_{*}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\nu\text{ and }1\leq l\leq d.\end{aligned}\right.

Now, our aim is to control
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
and
\|\rho_{*}\|
by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
, which also needs us to control
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1\right|
and the interaction between bubbles by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
.

In the second step, we need to expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
and the regular data
\mathcal{R}_{int}
of the equation of
\rho_{*}
to control
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1\right|
,
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
, the interaction between bubbles and
\|\rho_{*}\|
by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
, as in
[
22
]
. Roughly speaking, we shall decompose
\rho_{*}
into two parts, the first part is regular enough so that we can control it very well in any reasonable sense and the second part is (possible) singular due to the (possible) singularity of the data
f\in H^{-1}
but it can lie in the positive define part of the linear operator
\mathcal{L}
and is small enough. We notice that in the functional inequality setting, Frank and Peteranderl have proved in
[
53
]
that the optimal Bianchi-Egnell stability of the CKN inequality (
1.2
) in the degenerate case is quartic and the projection onto nontrivial kernel dominates the remaining term, thus, it is reasonable to expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
at least up to the fourth order terms and to ensure that the (possible) singular part of the remaining term
\rho_{*}
is smaller than
\max_{k,l}\left|\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right|^{4}
by decomposing the remaining term
\rho_{*}
in a suitable way. Keeping this in minds, we pick up all regular part of
\mathcal{R}_{int}+\mathcal{N}
which are potentially larger than or equal to
\max_{k,l}\left|\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right|^{4}

and solve several linear equations to decompose
\rho_{*}
into
\rho_{*}=\rho_{0}+\rho_{**}^{\perp}
, where
\rho_{0}
is the regular part and
\rho_{**}^{\perp}
is the (possible) singular part. We remark that to pick up all regular part of
\mathcal{R}_{int}+\mathcal{N}
which are potentially larger than or equal to
\max_{k,l}\left|\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right|^{4}
, we need to expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
up to the sixth order terms.

In the third step, we need to multiply the equation of
\rho_{*}
by
\{\Psi_{j}\}
,
\{\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\}
and
\{w_{j,l}\}
, and multiply the equation of
\rho_{**}^{\perp}
by
\rho_{**}^{\perp}
to establish the relations of
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1\right|
,
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
, the interaction between bubbles,
\|\rho_{*}\|
and
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
, as in
[
22
]
. However, these estimates are not good enough to finally control
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1\right|
,
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
, the interaction between bubbles and
\|\rho_{*}\|
only by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
. This is mainly because
\max_{k,l}\left|\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right|^{4}
can only be bounded from above by a very special form, as observed by Frank and Peteranderl in
[
53
]
. Thus, we need to find out the right third equation to march this special form and ensure that we will not enlarge the upper bounds in the original estimates of
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1\right|
,
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
, the interaction between bubbles,
\|\rho_{*}\|
and
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
in this progress. We remark that in order to ensure that the (possible) singular part of the remaining term
\rho_{*}
is smaller than
\max_{k,l}\left|\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right|^{4}
, we also need to full use the symmetry and the orthogonality.

In the final step, we use all above estimates of
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1\right|
,
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
, the interaction between bubbles,
\|\rho_{*}\|
and
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
and the estimates of
\max_{k,l}\left|\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right|^{4}
established by Frank and Peteranderl in
[
53
]
to derive the desired estimate in
(a)
of Theorem
1.3
. The proof of
(b)
of Theorem
1.3
is to construct an example by considering the case
\nu=2
and using the good ansatz
\sum_{j=1}^{2}\alpha_{j}^{*}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{j,l}^{*}w_{j,l}\right)+\rho_{0}
in the proof of
(a)
of Theorem
1.3
.

We believe that our strategy of proofs may be useful to study many other problems in which degeneracy appears.

1.4.
Structure of this paper

. In section 2, we give some preliminaries. In section 3, we introduce the setting of the problem as stated above by decomposing a given function into three parts, the projection on bubbles, the projection on nontrivial kernels and the remaining term. In section 4, we expand the nonlinear part of the remaining term up to the fourth order term in the first time to pick up all possible leading order terms in it and use these possible leading order terms to decompose the remaining term into the regular part and the (possible) singular part. In section 5, we refine the expansion of the nonlinear part by the decomposition of the remaining term by adding the regular part of the remaining term into the ansatz and estimates the differences of the projection on bubbles. In section 6, we expand the nonlinear part of the remaining term up to the sixth order term to estimate the interaction between bubbles. In section 7, we estimate the (possible) singular part. In section 8, we finally estimate the projection on nontrivial kernels and prove
(a)
of Theorem
1.3
. In section 9, we construct an optimal example and prove
(b)
of Theorem
1.3
.

2.
Preliminaries

The CKN inequality (
1.2
) can be rewritten as the following minimizing problem:

\displaystyle S_{a,b}^{-1}=\inf_{u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{\|u\|^{2}_{L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})}},

(2.1)

where
L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the usual weighted Lebesgue space and its usual norm is given by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p+1}(|x|^{-b(p+1)},\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p+1}dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p+1}}.

The Euler-Lagrange equation of the minimizing problem (
2.1
) is given by

\displaystyle-div(|x|^{-a}\nabla u)=|x|^{-b(p+1)}|u|^{p-1}u,\quad u\in D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.2)

It is well known (cf.
[
13
, Proposition 2.2]
) that
D^{1,2}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Hilbert space given by (
1.1
), is isomorphic to the Hilbert space
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
by the transformation

\displaystyle u(x)=|x|^{-(a_{c}-a)}v\left(-\ln|x|,\frac{x}{|x|}\right),

(2.3)

where we denote
a_{c}=\frac{d-2}{2}
as in
[
31
,
32
]
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the standard cylinder,
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
is the Hilbert space with the inner product given by

\displaystyle\langle w,v\rangle_{H^{1}(\mathcal{C})}=\int_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\nabla w\nabla v+(a_{c}-a)^{2}uv\right)d\mu

and
d\mu
the volume element on
\mathcal{C}
.
By (
2.3
), the minimizing problem (
2.1
) is equivalent to the following minimizing problem:

\displaystyle S_{a,b}^{-1}=\inf_{v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{C})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\|v\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\mathcal{C})}}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{p+1}(\mathcal{C})}},

(2.4)

where
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p+1}(\mathcal{C})}
is the usual norm in the Lebesgue space
L^{p+1}(\mathcal{C})
. For the sake of simplicity, we denote

\displaystyle L^{p+1}:=L^{p+1}(\mathcal{C})\quad\text{and}\quad H^{1}:=H^{1}(\mathcal{C})

in what follows. Let
t=-\ln|x|
and
\theta=\frac{x}{|x|}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\backslash\{0\}
, then the Euler-Lagrange equation of (
2.1
) in terms of
u
given by (
2.2
) is equivalent to the following Euler-Lagrange equation of (
2.4
) in terms of
v
:

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\theta}v-\partial_{t}^{2}v+(a_{c}-a)^{2}v=|v|^{p-1}v,\quad v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{C}),

(2.5)

where
\Delta_{\theta}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Clearly, minimizers of (
2.1
) are ground states of (
2.2
). Moreover,
by the transformation (
2.3
), the linear equation (
1.7
) can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\theta}v-\partial_{t}^{2}v+(a_{c}-a)^{2}v=p\Psi^{p-1}v,\quad v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{C}),

(2.6)

and
W(x)
given by (
1.4
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Psi(t)=\bigg(\frac{(p+1)(a_{c}-a)^{2}}{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\bigg(cosh\left(\frac{(a_{c}-a)(p-1)}{2}t\right)\bigg)^{-\frac{2}{p-1}}.

(2.7)

Since (
2.6
) is translational invariance, it follows from (
1.6
) and the transformation (
2.3
) that

\displaystyle\Psi_{s}^{\prime}(t)=\Psi^{\prime}(t-s)=\partial_{t}\Psi(t-s)=-\partial_{s}\Psi(t-s)

is the only nonzero solution of (
2.6
) in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
under the condition (
1.5
) except
b=b_{FS}(a)
.

For the special case
b=b_{FS}(a)
, the bubble
\Psi(t)
is degenerate in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
in the sense that the nonzero solution of (
2.6
) in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
is not only generated by the translational invariance of (
2.5
). Fortunately, we have the following lemma which provides a completely understanding of the solutions of the linear equation (
2.6
) in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
.

Lemma 2.1
.

(
[
53
, Lemma 7]
) Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then any solution of the linear equation (
2.6
) in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
is the linear combination of
\partial_{t}\Psi
and
\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{1},\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{2},\cdots,\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{d}
, where
\theta_{l}
are the standard spherical harmonics of degree
1
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Remark 2.1
.

As in
[
53
]
, we call
\partial_{t}\Psi
the trivial kernel of the the linear equation (
2.6
) in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
and call
\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{1},\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{2},\cdots,\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{d}
the nontrivial kernel of the linear equation (
2.6
) in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
. Moreover, since
\theta_{l}
are odd on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\partial_{t}\Psi
is odd in
\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi
is even in
\mathbb{R}
, by (
2.5
) and (
2.6
), we have the following orthogonal conditions:

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi,\partial_{t}\Psi\right\rangle=0,\quad\left\langle\Psi,w_{l}\right\rangle=0,\quad\left\langle\partial_{t}\Psi,w_{l}\right\rangle=0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle w_{j},w_{l}\right\rangle=0

for all
1\leq j\not=l\leq d
, where
w_{l}=\Psi^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{l}
.

3.
Setting of the problem

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then direct calculations show that

\displaystyle(a_{c}-a)^{2}=\frac{4(d-1)}{(p+1)^{2}-4}.

For the sake of simplicity, we denote that

\displaystyle S_{FS}:=S_{a,b}\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{FS}:=\frac{4(d-1)}{(p+1)^{2}-4}

for
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Let
v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
be a nonnegative function such that

\displaystyle\left(\nu-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(S_{FS}^{-1}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}<\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}<\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(S_{FS}^{-1}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}

for some positive integer
\nu\geq 2
and denote

\displaystyle f:=-\Delta_{\theta}v-\partial_{t}^{2}v+\Lambda_{FS}v-v^{p}.

(3.1)

Then it is easy to see that
f\in H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})
.

Since
a<b_{FS}(a)<a+1
for
a<0
and
d\geq 2
, by
[
13
, Lemma 4.2]
(see also
[
78
, Proposition 3.2]
), there exists
(\alpha_{1,\natural},\alpha_{2,\natural},\cdots,\alpha_{\nu,\natural})
and
(s_{1,\natural},s_{2,\natural},\cdots,s_{\nu,\natural})
satisfying

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq\nu}|\alpha_{j,\natural}-1|\to 0\text{ and }\min_{i\not=j}\left|s_{i,\natural}-s_{j,\natural}\right|\to+\infty\quad\text{as}\quad\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})}\to 0

such that

\displaystyle\left\|v-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j,\natural}\Psi_{s_{j,\natural}}\right\|^{2}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\|f\|_{H^{-1}}\to 0

(3.2)

where

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|^{2}=\|\cdot\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\Lambda_{FS}\|\cdot\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

is the equivalent norm in
H^{1}
. We also denote the related inner product in
H^{1}
, which is induced by the norm
\|\cdot\|
, by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
Thus, we can rewrite

\displaystyle v=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j,\natural}\Psi_{s_{j,\natural}}+\text{a remaining term}

in
H^{1}
as
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}\to 0
. To obtain an optimal decomposition as above, let us consider the following minimizing problem:

\displaystyle\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|v-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}\Psi_{s_{j}}\right\|^{2},

(3.3)

where
\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{\nu})
and
\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}=(s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{\nu})
. By (
3.2
) and similar arguments used for
[
78
, Proposition 4.1]
, we know that the variational problem (
3.3
) has minimizers, say
(\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}^{*},\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}^{*})
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq\nu}|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1|\to 0\text{ and }\min_{i\not=j}\left|s_{i}^{*}-s_{j}^{*}\right|\to+\infty\quad\text{as}\quad\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})}\to 0.

(3.4)

Thus, we can decompose

\displaystyle v=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}^{*}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}+\rho

(3.5)

where

\displaystyle\|\rho\|^{2}=\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|v-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}\Psi_{s_{j}}\right\|^{2}\to 0\quad\text{as }\|f\|_{H^{-1}}\to 0

(3.6)

and by the minimality of
(\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}^{*},\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}^{*})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\rho,\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}\right\rangle=0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle\rho,\partial_{t}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}\right\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }j=1,2,\cdots,\nu.

(3.7)

Since by Lemma
2.1
, the linear equation (
2.6
) has nontrivial kernels in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
for
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and and
b=b_{FS}(a)
, we need further decompose the remaining term as follows:

\displaystyle\rho=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{j,l}^{*}w_{j,l}\right)+\rho_{*},

(3.8)

where for the sake of simplicity, we denote
w_{j,l}=\Psi_{j}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\theta_{l}=w_{l}(t-s_{j}^{*})
and
\{\beta_{j,l}^{*}\}
is chosen such that
\left\langle w_{j,l},\rho_{*}\right\rangle=0
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and
1\leq l\leq d
. The above facts can be summarized into the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then we have the following decomposition of
v
:

\displaystyle v=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}^{*}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{j,l}^{*}w_{j,l}\right)+\rho_{*},

(3.9)

where the remaining term
\rho_{*}
satisfies the following orthogonal conditions:

\displaystyle\left\langle\rho_{*},\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}\right\rangle=0,\quad\left\langle\rho_{*},\partial_{t}\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}\right\rangle=0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle\rho_{*},w_{j,l}\right\rangle=0

(3.10)

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and
1\leq l\leq d
with

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq\nu}|\alpha_{j}^{*}-1|\to 0,\quad\min_{i\not=j}\left|s_{i}^{*}-s_{j}^{*}\right|\to+\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\|\rho_{*}\|\to 0

(3.11)

as
\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})}\to 0
. Moreover, we also have

\displaystyle\inf_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{\nu}\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{\nu},\overrightarrow{s}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\left\|v-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\alpha_{j}\Psi_{s_{j}}\right\|^{2}\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\left(\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right)^{2}+\|\rho_{*}\|^{2}.

(3.12)

Proof.

(
3.9
) can be obtained by (
3.5
) and (
3.8
), directly, while the orthogonal conditions of
\rho_{*}
is obtained by the choice of
\{\beta_{j,l}^{*}\}
, the orthogonal condition of
w_{j,l}
given in Remark
2.1
and the orthogonal condition of
\rho
given by (
3.7
). By (
3.4
), it remains to show that
\|\rho_{*}\|\to 0
as
\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})}\to 0
. Indeed, by the orthogonal condition of
w_{j,l}
given in Remark
2.1
and the orthogonal condition of
\rho
given by (
3.10
),

\displaystyle\|\rho\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\left(\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right)^{2}\left\|w_{d}\right\|^{2}+\|\rho_{*}\|^{2}

(3.13)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{m,n=1;m<n}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{n,l}^{*}\beta_{m,l}^{*}\left\langle w_{n,l},w_{m,l}\right\rangle,

where we have used the invariance of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and the norm
\|\cdot\|
under the action of orthogonal matrix
O(d)
. Clearly, by (
3.4
) and (
3.13
), it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\|\rho\|^{2}\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\nu}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\left(\beta_{k,l}^{*}\right)^{2}+\|\rho_{*}\|^{2},

which, together with (
3.6
), implies that
\|\rho_{*}\|\to 0
and (
3.12
) as
\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})}\to 0
.
∎

For the sake of simplicity, we use the notations
\Psi_{j}:=\Psi_{s_{j}^{*}}
,
\Psi_{j}^{*}=\alpha_{j}^{*}\Psi_{j}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{j}^{*},\quad\mathcal{U}_{j}=\mathcal{U}-\Psi_{j}^{*}=\sum_{i=1;i\not=j}^{\nu}\Psi_{i}^{*}

(3.14)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{j,l}^{*}w_{j,l}=\Psi_{j}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{j,l}^{*}\theta_{l},\quad\mathcal{V}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{V}_{j}.

(3.15)

Since
\Psi_{j}
are solutions of (
2.5
) and
w_{j,l}
are solutions of (
2.6
), by (
3.1
), (
3.8
) and (
3.10
), it is easy to see that the remaining term
\rho_{*}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}(\rho_{*})=f+\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2}+\mathcal{N},\quad\text{in }\mathcal{C},\\
&\langle\Psi_{j},\rho_{*}\rangle=\langle\partial_{t}\Psi_{j},\rho_{*}\rangle=\langle w_{j,l},\rho_{*}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\nu\text{ and }1\leq l\leq d,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.16)

where
\mathcal{L}(\rho_{*})
is the linear operator given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\rho_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{2}\rho_{*}-\Delta_{\theta}\rho_{*}+\Lambda_{FS}\rho_{*}-p\mathcal{U}^{p-1}\rho_{*}

(3.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\partial_{t}^{2}\rho_{*}-\Delta_{\theta}\rho_{*}+\Lambda_{FS}\rho_{*}-p\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-1}\rho_{*}\right)-p\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-1}-\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-1}\right)\rho_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{j}(\rho_{*})-\mathcal{L}_{j,ex}(\rho_{*})

for all
j=1,2,\cdots,\nu
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are the errors given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{p}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\left(\alpha_{j}^{*}\right)^{p}-\alpha_{j}^{*}\right)\Psi_{j}^{p}

(3.18)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{1,j}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-1}-\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-1}+\left((\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right)\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}

(3.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-1}-\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-1}\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}+p\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\left((\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right)\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{2,j},

and
\mathcal{N}
is the only nonlinear part of
\rho_{*}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\left(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{V}+\rho_{*}\right)^{p}-\mathcal{U}^{p}-p\mathcal{U}^{p-1}\left(\mathcal{V}+\rho_{*}\right).

(3.20)

By (
3.12
), to establish stability inequality of the CKN inequality in the critical point setting for
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
as in
[
22
,
40
,
78
]
, we shall control
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
and
\|\rho_{*}\|
by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
.

4.
first expansion of
\mathcal{N}
and further decomposition of
\rho_{*}

To get optimal control of
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
and
\|\rho_{*}\|
by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
, we shall
apply the ideas in
[
22
]
(see also
[
78
]
). Roughly speaking, we need to further decompose the remaining part
\rho_{*}
into two parts. The first part, say
\rho_{0}
, is regular enough in the sense that
\rho_{0}
can be controlled by a good weighted
L^{\infty}
norm. The second part, say
\rho_{**}^{\perp}
, is (possible) singular according to the (possible) lack of regularity of
f
which is much smaller than
\rho_{0}
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
. For this purpose, we need to expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
to pick up all possible leading order terms of the remaining part
\rho_{*}
.

4.1.
First expansion of
\mathcal{N}

Since by
[
53
, Theorem 1]
, the optimal Bianchi-Egnell stability of the CKN inequality for
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
is quartic, it is reasonable to first expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
up to the fourth order term.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then we have the following expansion of the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\left(\mathcal{V}^{2}+2\mathcal{V}\rho_{*}\right)+B_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\left(\mathcal{V}^{3}+3\mathcal{V}^{2}\rho_{*}\right)

(4.1)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p}\beta_{*}^{4}+\chi_{p\geq 2}|\rho_{*}|^{2}+|\rho_{*}|^{p}+\sum_{l=2}^{3}\beta_{*}^{\frac{2(l-p)_{+}}{p+1}}|\rho_{*}|^{l-\frac{2(l-p)_{+}}{p+1}}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{*}+\mathcal{N}_{rem}

in
\mathcal{C}
, where
\rho_{*}
,
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are given by (
3.9
), (
3.14
) and (
3.15
), respectively,
A_{p}=\frac{p(p-1)}{2}
,
B_{p}=\frac{p(p-1)(p-2)}{6}
and

\displaystyle\chi_{p\geq 2}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,\quad p\geq 2,\\
&0,\quad 1<p<2.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

As in the proof of
[
53
, Lemma 8]
, we introduce the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\left\{(\theta,t)\in\mathcal{C}\mid|\rho_{*}|\leq\left|\mathcal{V}\right|\right\}.

Note that by (
3.11
), (
3.15
) and
p>1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{V}\right|\lesssim\beta_{*}\mathcal{U}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\lesssim\beta_{*}\mathcal{U},

(4.2)

where
\beta_{*}=\max_{j,l}\left|\beta_{j,l}^{*}\right|
. Thus, we can apply the ideas in the proof of
[
53
, Lemma 8]
to expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{c}
, respectively, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\left(\mathcal{V}^{2}+2\mathcal{V}\rho_{*}\right)+B_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\left(\mathcal{V}^{3}+3\mathcal{V}^{2}\rho_{*}\right)

(4.3)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-4}\left(\mathcal{V}+\rho_{*}\right)^{4}+\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\left|\rho_{*}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\left(\mathcal{V}^{2}+2\mathcal{V}\rho_{*}\right)+B_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\left(\mathcal{V}^{3}+3\mathcal{V}^{2}\rho_{*}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathcal{U}^{3p-2}\beta_{*}^{4}+\chi_{p\geq 2}|\rho_{*}|^{2}+|\rho_{*}|^{p}\right)

in
\mathcal{A}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{O}\left(\chi_{p\geq 2}|\rho_{*}|^{2}+|\rho_{*}|^{p}\right)

(4.4)

in
\mathcal{A}^{c}
.
Since
\frac{2(2-p)}{p+1}\in(0,1)
for
1<p<2
and
\frac{2(3-p)}{p+1}\in(0,2)
for
1<p<3
, by (
3.14
) and (
3.15
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l=2}^{3}\beta_{*}^{\frac{2(l-p)_{+}}{p+1}}|\rho_{*}|^{l-\frac{2(l-p)_{+}}{p+1}}\gtrsim A_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\left(\mathcal{V}^{2}+2\left|\mathcal{V}\rho_{*}\right|\right)+B_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\left(\left|\mathcal{V}\right|^{3}+3\mathcal{V}^{2}|\rho_{*}|\right)

(4.5)

in
\mathcal{A}^{c}
, where
a_{\pm}=\max\{\pm a,0\}
. Thus, (
4.1
) is obtained by combining (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and (
4.5
).
∎

We need to further expand the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}_{*}
to separate the bubbles, for this purpose, we introduce some necessary notations. For the sake of simplicity and without loss of generality, we assume that

\displaystyle-\infty:=s_{0}<s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{\nu}<s_{\nu+1}:=+\infty.

We also denote

\displaystyle\tau_{j}=s_{j+1}-s_{j},\quad\tau=\min_{j=1,2,\cdots,\nu-1}\tau_{j}

(4.6)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[s_{1}^{*}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2},s_{1}^{*}+\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[s_{j}^{*}-\frac{\tau_{j-1}}{2},s_{j}^{*}+\frac{\tau_{j}}{2}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\quad 2\leq j\leq\nu-1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[s_{\nu}^{*}-\frac{\tau_{\nu-1}}{2},s_{\nu}^{*}+\frac{\tau_{\nu-1}}{2}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then the nonlinear part
\mathcal{N}
which is given by (
3.20
) can be further expanded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(A_{p}\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-2}\left(\mathcal{V}_{j}^{2}+2\mathcal{V}_{j}\rho_{*}\right)+B_{p}\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-3}\left(\mathcal{V}_{j}^{3}+3\mathcal{V}_{j}^{2}\rho_{*}\right)\right)\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}

(4.7)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}2A_{p}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\mathcal{V}-\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-2}\mathcal{V}_{j}\right)\rho_{*}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{*}^{2}\mathcal{U}_{j}\Psi_{j}^{2p-3}\left(\Psi_{j}+\rho_{*}\right)\right)\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}

\displaystyle+\left(2A_{p}\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\mathcal{V}\rho_{*}+\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\mathcal{V}^{2}\left(A_{p}\mathcal{U}+B_{p}\mathcal{V}\right)\right)\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)}+\mathcal{N}_{rem}

in
\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{rem}
is given in (
4.1
) and
A_{p}
,
B_{p}
are given in Lemma
4.1
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{U}_{j}>0
in
\mathcal{C}
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
by (
3.14
), by (
2.7
), (
3.15
) and the Taylor expansion, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\Psi_{j}^{p-\alpha-1}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha-1}\mathcal{U}_{j}+\Psi_{j}^{p-\alpha}\left|\sum_{i=1;i\not=j}^{\nu}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha-2}\mathcal{V}_{i}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{*}^{\alpha-1}\left(\Psi_{j}^{\frac{\alpha(p-1)+p-3}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{j}+\Psi_{j}^{\frac{\alpha(p-1)-2}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{j}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\right)\right)

in
\mathcal{B}_{j}
for all
j=1,2,\cdots,\nu
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha-1}=\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{*}^{\alpha-1}\mathcal{U}^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(p-1)}{2}}\right)

in
\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)
, where
\alpha=2
or
\alpha=3
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}\left(\Psi_{j}^{p-\alpha-1}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{j}+\Psi_{j}^{p-\alpha}\left|\sum_{i=1;i\not=j}^{\nu}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha-1}\mathcal{V}_{i}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{*}^{\alpha}\left(\Psi_{j}^{\frac{\alpha(p-1)}{2}+p-1}\mathcal{U}_{j}+\Psi_{j}^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(p-1)}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{j}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\right)\right)

in
\mathcal{B}_{j}
for all
j=1,2,\cdots,\nu
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{*}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}^{\frac{\alpha(p-1)}{2}+p}\right)

in
\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)
. Thus, summarizing the above estimates of
\mathcal{U}^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha-1}
and
\mathcal{U}^{p-\alpha}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{N}_{*}
and by
p>1
and Lemma
4.1
, we have the desired expansion of
\mathcal{N}
given by (
4.7
), where
\mathcal{N}_{rem}
is given in (
4.1
).
∎

4.2.
Further decomposition of
\rho_{*}

Recall that we shall control
\{\beta_{k,l}^{*}\}
and
\|\rho_{*}\|
by
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
. However, due to the errors
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, we have two additional terms which need to control. The first one is
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\left(\alpha_{j}^{*}\right)^{p}-\alpha_{j}^{*}\right)
, which the (possible) difference of the decomposition of
v
given in Lemma
3.1
on the bubbles. The second one is the interaction between bubbles. To measure the interaction between bubbles, we denote

\displaystyle Q_{j}=e^{-\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}\tau_{j}},\quad\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}(t)=e^{-\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}|t-s^{*}_{i}|}\quad\text{and}\quad Q=e^{-\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}\tau}

(4.8)

where
i,j=1,2,\cdots,\nu
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then for every
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha+\beta\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beta}d\mu\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &Q^{\beta},\quad\alpha>\beta,\\
&Q^{\beta}\left|\log Q\right|,\quad\alpha=\beta,\\
&Q^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}},\quad\alpha<\beta\end{aligned}\right.

(4.9)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{l=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{l}\right)}\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\Psi_{j}^{\beta}d\mu\lesssim Q^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}}.

(4.10)

Proof.

Recall that

\displaystyle s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{\nu-1}<s_{\nu},

thus, by (
2.7
), (
4.6
), (
4.8
) and similar estimates for (
4.7
), we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beta}\sim e^{-\alpha\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(t-s^{*}_{i})}e^{-\beta\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(s^{*}_{i+1}-t)}\sim Q_{i}^{\beta}e^{-(\alpha-\beta)\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(t-s^{*}_{i})}

(4.11)

in the region

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,+}:=\left[s^{*}_{i},s^{*}_{i}+\frac{\tau_{i}}{2}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.12)

for all
i=1,2,\cdots,\nu-1
,
while in the region

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,-}:=\left[s_{i}^{*}-\frac{\tau_{i-1}}{2},s_{i}^{*}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.13)

for all
i=2,3,\cdots,\nu
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beta}\sim e^{-\alpha\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(s^{*}_{i}-t)}e^{-\beta\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(t-s^{*}_{i-1})}\sim Q_{i-1}^{\beta}e^{-(\alpha-\beta)\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(s^{*}_{i}-t)}.

(4.14)

Thus, by direct calculations, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beta}d\mu

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,+}}\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\Psi_{i+1}^{\beta}d\mu+\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,-}}\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\Psi_{i-1}^{\beta}d\mu

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &Q^{\beta},\quad\alpha>\beta,\\
&Q^{\beta}\left|\log Q\right|,\quad\alpha=\beta,\\
&Q^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}}\quad\alpha<\beta,\end{aligned}\right.

which implies that (
4.9
) holds true. To prove (
4.10
), we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1,-,*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\infty,s_{1}^{*}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\infty,s_{1}^{*}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\right)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cup\left[s_{1}^{*}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2},s_{1}^{*}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}\cup\mathcal{B}_{1,-},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+,*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[s_{\nu}^{*},+\infty\right)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[s_{\nu}^{*},s_{\nu}^{*}+\frac{\tau_{\nu-1}}{2}\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cup\left(s_{\nu}^{*}+\frac{\tau_{\nu-1}}{2},+\infty\right)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+}\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}.

Then by (
2.7
), (
4.6
) and similar estimates for (
4.7
),
we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}^{\alpha}\Psi_{j}^{\beta}\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &e^{-(\alpha+\beta)\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(s^{*}_{1}-t)},\quad\text{in }\mathcal{B}_{1,-,*},\\
&e^{-(\alpha+\beta)\sqrt{\Lambda_{FS}}(t-s^{*}_{\nu})},\quad\text{in }\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+,*}.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.17)

Thus, (
4.10
) is also obtained by direct calculations.
∎

To further decompose the remaining part
\rho_{*}
and pick up the leading order term,
let us first consider the following equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{1,ex})=\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\left(c_{1,ex,j}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{d}\varsigma_{1,ex,j,l}w_{i,l}\right),\quad\text{in }\mathcal{C},\\
&\langle\partial_{t}\Psi_{j},\gamma_{1,ex}\rangle=\langle w_{j,l},\gamma_{1,ex}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\nu\text{ and all }1\leq l\leq d,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.18)

where
\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}
is given by (
3.18
).

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then (
4.18
) is uniquely solvable. Moreover, the solution
\gamma_{1,ex}
in even on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and satisfies

\displaystyle 1\gtrsim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\|\gamma_{1,ex}\|_{\sharp},\quad p\geq 3,\\
&\|\gamma_{1,ex}\|_{\natural,1,*},\quad 1<p<3,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.19)

where the Lagrange multipliers
\{c_{1,ex,j}\}
and
\{\varsigma_{1,ex,j,l}\}
are chosen such that the right hand side of the equation (
4.18
) is orthogonal to
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\}
and
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C})
,

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{1,ex}\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{\nu-1}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}+\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{1}\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q\varphi_{s^{*}_{1}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q\varphi_{s^{*}_{\nu}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

and

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{1,ex}\|_{\natural,1,*}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu-1}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q_{i}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{p-2}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{\nu,0}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q_{\nu}\varphi_{s^{*}_{\nu}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q_{i-1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{p-2}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{1,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{1,0}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q_{1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{1}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,0}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,ex}|}{Q}

with
\sigma>0
being a small constant which can be taken arbitrary small if necessary and

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,0}=[s_{i}^{*}-1,s_{i}^{*}+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cup\left[\frac{s_{i}^{*}+s_{i+1}^{*}}{2}-1,\frac{s_{i}^{*}+s_{i+1}^{*}}{2}+1\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

The Lagrange multipliers also satisfy
|c_{1,ex,j}|\lesssim Q
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and
\varsigma_{1,ex,j,l}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
.

Proof.

By (
3.18
) and similar estimates for (
4.11
), (
4.14
) and (
4.17
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}\sim\left\{\begin{aligned} &Q_{i}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{p-2}(t),\quad\mathcal{B}_{i,+}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq\nu-1,\\
&Q_{i-1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i-1}}^{p-2}(t),\quad\mathcal{B}_{i,-}\text{ for }2\leq i\leq\nu,\\
&Q_{1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{1}}^{p}(t),\quad\mathcal{B}_{1,-,*},\\
&Q_{\nu}\varphi_{s^{*}_{\nu}}^{p}(t),\quad\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+,*},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.20)

where
\mathcal{B}_{i,+}
,
\mathcal{B}_{i,-}
,
\mathcal{B}_{1,-,*}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+,*}
are given by (
4.12
), (
4.13
), (
4.15
) and (
4.16
).
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}\|_{\natural,1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu-1}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q_{i}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{p-2}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{\nu,0,*}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q_{\nu}\varphi_{s^{*}_{\nu}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

(4.21)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q_{i-1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{p-2}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{1,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{1,0,*}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q_{1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{1}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1

for
1<p<3
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}=[s_{i}^{*}-2,s_{i}^{*}+2]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\left[\frac{s_{i}^{*}+s_{i+1}^{*}}{2}-2,\frac{s_{i}^{*}+s_{i+1}^{*}}{2}+2\right]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.22)

and

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{\nu-1}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}+\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{1}\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

(4.23)

\displaystyle+\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}|}{Q\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1

for
p\geq 3
, where
\sigma>0
is a small constant which can be taken arbitrary small.
Since
it is easy to check that
\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{1-\sigma}(t)
for all
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{p-2}(t)
for all
p\in(1,3)
are supersolutions of the equation
\mathcal{L}(\rho)=0
in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq\nu
, by Lemma
2.1
and applying similar cut-off functions and blow-up arguments for
[
64
, Proposition 3.1]
to (
4.18
) and by (
4.21
) and (
4.23
), we can show the existence and uniqueness of
\gamma_{1,ex}
with the desired estimates (
4.19
). Moreover, since
\mathcal{R}_{1,ex}
is even on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, by uniqueness, we also have that
\gamma_{1,ex}
is even on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. It remains to estimate the Lagrange multipliers. By the orthogonal conditions and the oddness of
w_{j,l}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}c_{1,ex,i}=-\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{1,ex},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\nu}\sum_{n=1}^{d}\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\varsigma_{1,ex,m,n}=0

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
. Since the matrix
\left[\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right]
is diagonally dominant by (
3.11
), by
p>1
, Lemma
4.3
and (
4.20
),

\displaystyle|c_{1,ex,j}|

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\left|\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{1,ex},\Psi_{l}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|

(4.24)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,+}}Q_{i}\Psi_{i}^{p-2}\Psi_{l}^{p}d\mu+\sum_{i=l+1}^{\nu}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,-}}Q_{i-1}\Psi_{i}^{p-2}\Psi_{l}^{p}d\mu

\displaystyle+p\int_{\mathcal{B}_{l}}\Psi_{l}^{2p-1}l\mathcal{U}_{l}d\mu

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{j,+}}\Psi_{j}^{2p-1}\Psi_{j+1}d\mu+\int_{\mathcal{B}_{j,-}}\Psi_{j}^{2p-1}\Psi_{j-1}d\mu

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle Q

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
.
Moreover, by (
3.11
) and the orthogonal conditions of
\{w_{l}\}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the matrix
\left[\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right]
is also diagonally dominant. Thus, it is also easy to see that
\varsigma_{1,ex,j,l}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
.
∎

We next consider the following equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{1,j})=\mathcal{R}_{1,j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\left(c_{1,j,i}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i}+\sum_{l=1}^{d}\varsigma_{1,j,i,l}w_{i,l}\right),\quad\text{in }\mathcal{C},\\
&\langle\partial_{t}\Psi_{j},\gamma_{1,j}\rangle=\langle w_{j,l},\gamma_{1,j}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\nu\text{ and all }1\leq l\leq d,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.25)

where
\mathcal{R}_{1,j}
is given by (
3.18
).

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then (
4.25
) is uniquely solvable. Moreover, the solution
\gamma_{1,j}
is even in terms of
t-s_{j}^{*}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|\gtrsim\sup_{(t,\theta)\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{|\gamma_{1,j}|}{\varphi_{s_{j}^{*}}^{1-\sigma}(t)},

(4.26)

where the Lagrange multipliers
\{c_{1,j,i}\}
and
\{\varsigma_{1,j,i,l}\}
are chosen such that the right hand side of the equation (
4.25
) is orthogonal to
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\}
and
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C})
and
\sigma>0
is chosen such that

\displaystyle\left(Q+\beta_{*}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|\right)\leq\frac{1}{8}\mathcal{U}^{\sigma}\text{ in }\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{*}=[s_{1}^{*}-\tau_{1},s_{\nu}^{*}+\tau_{\nu-1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

(4.27)

The Lagrange multipliers also satisfy
\varsigma_{1,j,i,l}=0
and

\displaystyle\left|c_{1,j,i}\right|\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|Q^{p}\left|\log Q\right|,\quad i\not=j,\\
&\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|Q^{2p}\left|\log Q\right|^{2},\quad i=j,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
,
1\leq l\leq d
and all
1\leq i\leq\nu
.

Proof.

Again, since
p>1
, by Lemma
2.1
and applying similar blow-up arguments for
[
78
, Lemma 5.4]
to (
4.25
) and by (
2.7
) and (
3.18
), we can show the existence and uniqueness of
\gamma_{1,j}
with the desired estimates (
4.26
). Moreover, since
\mathcal{R}_{1,j}
is even in terms of
t-s_{j}^{*}
, by uniqueness,
\gamma_{1,j}
is also even in terms of
t-s_{j}^{*}
. For the estimates of the Lagrange multipliers, by the orthogonal conditions and the oddness of
w_{j,l}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}c_{1,j,i}=-\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{1,j},\Psi_{l}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\nu}\sum_{n=1}^{d}\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\varsigma_{1,j,m,n}=0

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
. Again, the matrix
\left[\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right]
is diagonally dominant by (
3.11
). Note that by the oddness of
\partial_{t}\Psi
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{1,j},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

Thus,

\displaystyle\left|c_{1,j,i}\right|\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|\int_{\mathcal{C}}\Psi_{i}^{p}\Psi_{j}^{p}d\mu\lesssim\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|Q^{p}\left|\log Q\right|,\quad i\not=j,\\
&\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|\left(\int_{\mathcal{C}}\Psi_{i}^{p}\Psi_{j}^{p}d\mu\right)^{2}\lesssim\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|Q^{2p}\left|\log Q\right|^{2},\quad i=j,\end{aligned}\right.

for all
1\leq i\leq\nu
.
Moreover, by (
3.11
) and the orthogonal conditions of
\{w_{l}\}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the matrix
\left[\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right]
is also diagonally dominant. Thus,
it is also easy to see that
\varsigma_{1,j,i,l}=0

for all
1\leq i,j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
.
∎

We also need to consider the following equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}(\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex})=\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\left(c_{2,ex,j}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{d}\varsigma_{2,ex,j,l}w_{j,l}\right),\quad\text{in }\mathcal{C},\\
&\langle\partial_{t}\Psi_{j},\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}\rangle=\langle w_{j,l},\overline{\gamma}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\nu\text{ and all }1\leq l\leq d,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.28)

where
\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}
is given by (
3.19
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then (
4.28
) is uniquely solvable. Moreover, the solution
\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}
is odd on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\beta_{*}\gtrsim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}\|_{\sharp},\quad p\geq\frac{7}{3},\\
&\|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}\|_{\natural,2,*},\quad 1<p<\frac{7}{3},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.29)

where the Lagrange multipliers
\{c_{2,ex,j}\}
and
\{\varsigma_{2,ex,j,l}\}
are chosen such that the right hand side of the equation (
4.28
) is orthogonal to
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\}
and
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C})
,
\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
and
\mathcal{B}_{i,0}
are given in Lemma
4.4
, and

\displaystyle\|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}\|_{\natural,2,*}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu-1}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0}}\frac{|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{i}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{\frac{3p-5}{2}}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{\nu,0}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}}\frac{|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{\nu}\varphi_{s^{*}_{\nu}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0}}\frac{|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{i-1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{\frac{3p-5}{2}}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{1,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{1,0,*}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}}\frac{|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{1}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,0}}\frac{|\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}|}{Q}

with
\sigma>0
being a small constant which can be taken arbitrary small if necessary.
The Lagrange multipliers also satisfy
c_{2,ex,j}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and
|\varsigma_{2,ex,j,l}|\lesssim\beta_{*}Q
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and
1\leq l\leq d
.

Proof.

Similar to (
4.7
), by (
2.7
), (
3.15
) and (
3.19
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}\right|\lesssim\beta_{*}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{i}^{\frac{3(p-1)}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{i}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}+\mathcal{U}^{\frac{3p-1}{2}}\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)}\right).

(4.30)

Thus, similar to (
4.21
) and (
4.23
),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}\|_{\natural,2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu-1}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{i}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{\frac{3p-5}{2}}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{\nu,+}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{\nu,0,*}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{+}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{\nu}\varphi_{s^{*}_{\nu}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

(4.31)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{i-1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{i}}^{\frac{3p-5}{2}}(t)}+\sup_{\left(\mathcal{B}_{1,-}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{1,0,*}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{C}\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)^{-}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}|}{Q_{1}\varphi_{s^{*}_{1}}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\sup_{\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}}\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}|}{Q}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\beta_{*}

for
1<p<\frac{7}{3}
and
\|\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}\|_{\sharp}\lesssim\beta_{*}

for
p\geq\frac{7}{3}
, where
\sigma>0
is a small constant which can be taken arbitrary small if necessary and
\mathcal{B}_{i,0,*}
is given by (
4.22
). Then by Lemma
2.1
and applying similar cut-off functions and blow-up arguments for
[
64
, Proposition 3.1]
to (
4.28
), we can show the existence and uniqueness of
\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}
with the desired estimates (
4.29
). On the other hand, by (
3.15
) and (
3.19
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2,ex}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-1}-\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-1}\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{d}\left(p\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\mathcal{U}^{p-1}-\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-1}\right)\Psi_{j}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\beta_{j,l}^{*}\right)\theta_{l},

which is odd on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Thus, by uniqueness of
\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}
, we know that
\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}
is also odd on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
It remains to estimate the Lagrange multipliers. By the orthogonal conditions and the oddness of
w_{j,l}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}c_{2,ex,i}=0

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\nu}\sum_{n=1}^{d}\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\varsigma_{2,ex,m,n}=-\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{2,ex},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and
1\leq l\leq d
. Again, the matrix
\left[\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right]
is diagonally dominant by (
3.11
). Thus,
c_{2,ex,j}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
. Moreover, the matrix
\left[\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right]
is also diagonally dominant by (
3.11
) and the orthogonal conditions of
\{w_{l}\}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Thus, by Lemma
4.3
and (
4.30
), we also have

\displaystyle\left|\varsigma_{2,ex,j,l}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{2,ex},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\beta_{*}\left\langle\Psi_{j-1}^{\frac{3(p-1)}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{j-1}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j-1,+}}+\Psi_{j+1}^{\frac{3(p-1)}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{j+1}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j+1,-}},\Psi_{j}^{\frac{3p-1}{2}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\beta_{*}\left\langle\Psi_{j}^{3p-2},\mathcal{U}_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{j})}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\beta_{*}Q.

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
.
∎

We finally consider the following equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*})=\mathcal{N}_{led}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\left(c_{\mathcal{N},led,j}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{d}\varsigma_{\mathcal{N},led,j,l}w_{j,l}\right),\quad\text{in }\mathcal{C},\\
&\langle\partial_{t}\Psi_{j},\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}\rangle=\langle w_{j,l},\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\nu\text{ and all }1\leq l\leq d,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.32)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{led}=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left(\Psi_{j}^{*}\right)^{p-3}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{2}\left(A_{p}\Psi_{j}^{*}+B_{p}\mathcal{V}_{j}\right)\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}+\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\mathcal{V}^{2}\left(A_{p}\mathcal{U}+B_{p}\mathcal{V}\right)\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)}.

(4.33)

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
a<0
and
b=b_{FS}(a)
. Then (
4.32
) is uniquely solvable. Moreover, the solution
\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}
satisfies

\displaystyle\beta_{*}^{2}\gtrsim\sup_{(t,\theta)\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{|\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}|}{\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\Psi_{j}^{1-\sigma}(t)}

(4.34)

where the Lagrange multipliers
\{c_{\mathcal{N},led,j}\}
and
\{\varsigma_{\mathcal{N},led,j,l}\}
are chosen such that the right hand side of the equation (
4.32
) is orthogonal to
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\}
and
\{\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C})
and
\sigma>0
is chosen to satisfy (
4.27
).
The Lagrange multipliers also satisfy
|c_{\mathcal{N},led,j}|\lesssim\beta_{*}^{2}Q^{p}
and
\left|\varsigma_{\mathcal{N},led,j,l}\right|\lesssim\beta_{*}^{3}

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
,
1\leq l\leq d
and
1\leq i\leq\nu
.

Proof.

Similar to (
4.30
), by (
4.35
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{N}_{led}\right|\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\beta_{*}^{2}\Psi_{j}^{2p-1}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}+\beta_{*}^{2}\mathcal{U}^{2p-1}\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)}.

(4.35)

Since
p>1
, by Lemma
2.1
and applying similar blow-up arguments for
[
78
, Lemma 5.4]
to (
4.32
) and by (
2.7
), we can show the existence of
\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}
with the desired estimates (
4.34
).
It remains to estimate the Lagrange multipliers. By the oddness of
w_{l}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
\partial_{t}\Psi
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\left\langle\Psi_{i}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{i},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}c_{\mathcal{N},led,i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\left(\Psi_{i}^{*}\right)^{p-2}\mathcal{V}_{i}^{2}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}+\mathcal{U}^{p-2}\mathcal{V}^{2}\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\nu}\sum_{n=1}^{d}\left\langle\Psi_{m}^{p-1}w_{m,n},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\varsigma_{\mathcal{N},led,m,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\left(\Psi_{i}^{*}\right)^{p-3}\mathcal{V}_{i}^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}+\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\mathcal{V}^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)},\Psi_{j}^{p-1}w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
.
Thus, similar to (
4.24
), by Lemma
4.3
and the oddness of
\partial_{t}\Psi
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle|c_{\mathcal{N},led,j}|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{j-1}}\Psi_{j-1}^{p-2}\mathcal{V}_{j-1}^{2}\Psi_{j}^{p}d\mu+\int_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}\Psi_{j}^{p-2}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{2}\Psi_{j}^{p-1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{j}d\mu

(4.36)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\beta_{*}^{2}Q^{p}+\beta_{*}^{2}\int_{s_{j}^{*}+\frac{\tau}{2}}^{s_{j}^{*}+\frac{\tau_{j}}{2}}\Psi_{j}^{3p-1}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\beta_{*}^{2}Q^{p}

and by the oddness of
w_{l}
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\varsigma_{\mathcal{N},led,j,l}\right|\lesssim\left|\left\langle\Psi_{j}^{p-3}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}+\mathcal{U}^{p-3}\mathcal{V}^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{C}\backslash\left(\cup_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)},w_{j,l}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|\lesssim\beta_{*}^{3}

(4.37)

for all
1\leq j\leq\nu
and all
1\leq l\leq d
.
∎

By Lemmas
4.4
,
4.5
,
4.6
and
4.7
, we have picked up all possible leading order terms of
\rho_{*}
in terms of
Q
,
\beta_{*}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\left|(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{p-1}-1\right|
. Now, let

\displaystyle\rho_{**,0}=\rho_{*}-\gamma_{1,ex}-\overline{\gamma}_{2,ex}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\gamma_{1,j}-\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}.

Since
\gamma_{1,ex}
,
\gamma_{1,j}
and
\gamma_{\mathcal{N},led,*}
